任务层面的统一相关研究地址查找

科研·论文调研 · 24 条消息 · 来源：豆包分享
我问

任务层面的统一 (Task-Level Unification)：早期的 Palette 用一个条件扩散模型框架，统一了上色、修复、扩展和JPEG恢复等多种图像到图像的任务。近期的 His-MMDM 则将扩散框架扩展到了病理图像的多域、多组学翻译，统一了冷冻切片到FFPE、虚拟IHC染色等多种任务。这类工作统一的是任务接口，而非结构保持机制。这个你给我找一下地址

1. Palette: Image-to-Image Diffusion Models

对应你描述：上色(colorization)、修复(inpainting)、扩展(uncropping)、JPEG恢复，条件扩散统一多图像到图像任务
  • arXiv预印本：https://arxiv.org/pdf/2111.05826v2.pdf
  • 论文主页：https://diffusionpalette.github.io/
  • SIGGRAPH 2022正式版：https://dl.acm.org/doi/10.1145/3528233.3530757

2. His-MMDM: Multi-Domain and Multi-Omics Translation of Histopathological Images with Diffusion Models

对应：冷冻切片→FFPE、虚拟IHC染色，病理图像多域+多组学翻译扩散模型
  • medRxiv预印本：https://www.medrxiv.org/content/10.1101/2024.07.11.24310294v2
  • PMC（Advanced Science 期刊在线版）：https://pmc.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/articles/PMC13045368/
  • Github代码仓库：https://github.com/lzx325/His-MMDM

如果你需要，我可以直接把两条BibTeX引用一次性整理好。

我问

这两个分别都是什么工作啊

一、Palette: Image-to-Image Diffusion Models（SIGGRAPH 2022）

一句话定位：第一个用同一个条件扩散模型，统一4类图像到图像翻译任务的通用图像生成框架

  • 核心思想：不针对每个任务单独设计网络结构、单独调参，只用一套条件扩散模型（U-Net，输入原图作为条件，concat到输入），一个模型搞定4个图像翻译任务：
  1. 灰度图上色（colorization）
  2. 图像修复（inpainting，补图像缺失孔洞）
  3. 图像外扩（uncropping，向外扩展画面）
  4. JPEG压缩伪影恢复（JPEG restoration）
  • 背景：在Palette之前，图像翻译大多是GAN，一个任务训练一个专属模型；Palette证明单模型多任务图像到图像扩散是可行的，不需要为每个任务改网络结构。
  • 关键点（对应你原文描述）：统一的是任务接口，不是结构保持机制。模型只负责“根据输入图生成目标图”，没有专门约束要保留原图的底层结构，只是靠扩散生成来做图像翻译。
  • 局限：自然图像，不涉及病理；只能图像条件，没有多组学条件。

二、His-MMDM：Multi-Domain and Multi-Omics Translation of Histopathological Images with Diffusion Models（Advanced Science，2024）

一句话定位：把Palette这种“单模型统一多图像翻译”的思路，迁移到病理图像，并且扩展到多域+多组学条件的扩散模型

  • 核心思想：基于DDIB（去噪扩散隐式桥），一个扩散模型支持任意多的病理域之间的图像翻译，不再是传统GAN只能一对一对域翻译。
  • 能做的任务：
  1. 冷冻切片 → FFPE石蜡切片（不同制片方式的病理图像转换）
  2. 虚拟IHC免疫组化染色（H&E切片转IHC，虚拟染色）
  3. 跨肿瘤类型图像翻译、原发灶↔转移灶图像转换
  4. 多组学驱动编辑：输入基因突变/转录组数据，看“如果这个基因发生突变，组织切片会长成什么样子”（这是Palette完全没有的能力）
  • 关键点（和Palette对比）：
  • 继承Palette思想：统一一套扩散模型，统一任务接口，不用每个翻译任务单独搭模型；
  • 创新：条件不止图像，还可以是域标签、基因组/转录组多组学向量；
  • 同样：没有专门设计结构保持机制，靠条件扩散做分布映射，不是显式约束组织细胞结构。

两者对比（贴合你那段综述原文）

PaletteHis-MMDM
领域自然图像病理组织图像
基础模型条件扩散DDIB扩散模型
统一的任务上色、修复、外扩、JPEG恢复冷冻切片↔FFPE、虚拟IHC、跨肿瘤翻译、多组学图像编辑
条件输入图像图像 + 域标签 + 基因组/转录组多组学
共性任务层面统一，统一接口，无显式结构保持机制
你的原文那句话的含义： Palette、His-MMDM这类工作，是把很多不同的图像翻译任务，塞进同一个扩散模型的接口里，输入条件图，输出目标图； 但是模型本身没有专门模块去强制保证细胞/图像底层解剖结构严格不变，只是做分布层面的图像翻译，这就是“统一任务接口，而非结构保持机制”。

His-MMDM: Multi-domain and Multi-omics Translation of Histopathological Images with Diffusion Models Zhongxiao Li1,2,#, Tianqi Su3,#, Bin Zhang1,2, Wenkai Han1,2, Sibin Zhang3, Guiyin Sun3, Yuwei Cong4, Xin Chen5, Jiping Qi4, Yujie Wang3, Shiguang Zhao5,, Hongxue Meng6,, Peng Liang3,, and Xin Gao1,2, 1Computer Science Program, Computer, Electrical and Mathematical Sciences and Engineering Division, King Abdullah University of Science and Technology (KAUST), Thuwal, Saudi Arabia 2Center of Excellence on Smart Health, King Abdullah University of Science and Technology (KAUST), Thuwal, Saudi Arabia 3Department of Neurosurgery, Harbin Medical University Cancer Hospital, Harbin, China 4Department of Pathology, The First Affiliated Hospital of Harbin Medical University, Harbin, China 5Department of Neurosurgery, The First Affiliated Hospital of Harbin Medical University, Harbin, China 6Department of Pathology, Harbin Medical University Cancer Hospital, Harbin, China #Equal Contribution Meng All correspondence should be addressed to Shiguang Zhao (E-mail: guangsz@hotmail.com), Hongxue (E-mail: menghongxue@hrbmu.edu.cn), Peng Liang (E-mail: liangpeng@hrbmu.edu.cn) and Xin Gao (E-mail: xin.gao@kaust.edu.sa, Tel: +966-12-8080323) Page 1 of 35 NOTE: This preprint reports new research that has not been certified by peer review and should not be used to guide clinical practice. Page 2 of 35 Abstract Generative AI (GenAI) has advanced computational pathology through various image translation 1 models. These models synthesize histopathological images from existing ones, facilitating tasks 2 such as color normalization and virtual staining. Current models, while effective, are mostly 3 dedicated to specific source-target domain pairs and lack scalability for multi-domain translations. 4 Here we introduce His-MMDM, a diffusion model-based framework enabling multi-domain and 5 multi-omics histopathological image translation. His-MMDM is not only effective in performing 6 existing tasks such as transforming cryosectioned images to FFPE ones and virtual 7 immunohistochemical (IHC) staining but can also facilitate knowledge transfer between different 8 tumor types and between primary and metastatic tumors. Additionally, it performs genomics- 9 and/or transcriptomics-guided editing of histopathological images, illustrating the impact of driver 10 mutations and oncogenic pathway alterations on tissue histopathology and educating pathologists 11 to recognize them. These versatile capabilities position His-MMDM as a versatile tool in the GenAI 12 toolkit for future pathologists. 13 Keywords Image Generative Models, Image Translation, Diffusion Models, Histopathology . CC-BY-NC-ND 4.0 International license It is made available under a is the author/funder, who has granted medRxiv a license to display the preprint in perpetuity. (which was not certified by peer review) The copyright holder for this preprint this version posted November 1, 2024. ; https://doi.org/10.1101/2024.07.11.24310294 doi: medRxiv preprint Page 3 of 35 Introduction 14 Generative artificial intelligence (GenAI) in imagery has marked a groundbreaking advance in the 15 field of artificial intelligence (AI), offering transformative capabilities across a wide range of 16 applications. At the forefront, the state-of-the-art text-to-image models such as Stable Diffusion 1, 17 Midjourney 2, and DALL-E 3 3, transform text descriptions into vivid visual representations. 18 Conversely, image-to-text models, such as GPT-4V 4, interpret and convert visual data into 19 descriptive text, enabling open-ended dialogs about the image content with users. There are also 20 image-to-image translation models that use existing images as templates to generate new ones, 21 enabling applications such as image editing, resolution enhancement, and sketch-to-image 22 synthesis 1,5. Collectively, these models are revolutionizing the way we create, interpret, and 23 interact with visual contents. 24 Such a revolution is also gradually taking place in the field of computational pathology. Early 25 explorations have demonstrated the effectiveness of using generative adversarial networks (GANs) 26 6 or diffusion models (DMs) 7 to generate synthetic histopathological images 8-10, and have been 27 mainly used as a data augmentation strategy 11-13. Recently, a lot of efforts have been devoted to 28 improving the quality and enlarging the resolution of the generated images 14,15. Concurrently, 29 visual language foundation models and copilot systems are also being developed in computational 30 pathology to automate medical visual question answering 16-20. As for image-to-image translation, 31 dedicated models have been developed for various common tasks such as color normalization 21, 32 stain transfer 22, virtual staining 22-25, and transforming cryo-sectioned or stimulated Raman images 33 to formalin-fixed paraffin-embedded (FFPE) ones 26,27. 34 In general, there are two types of image-to-image translation tasks, including the paired and 35 unpaired ones. The paired image translation task requires matched source and target domain images 36 during training, which is usually not readily available 28. In contrast, the unpaired image translation 37 task requires the algorithm to learn the mapping between a source and target domain on itself, 38 making it more useful in real-world applications 28. It is thus not surprising that almost all the 39 existing image translation models in computational pathology are un-paired. Although the 40 abovementioned image translation models serve as important computational pathology 41 applications, their full potential still has not yet been unleashed. Firstly, current applications are 42 mostly limited to GANs, whereas recent developments of DMs show their superior performance 43 on conditional generation as compared to GANs 29. Secondly, the current GAN-based 44 histopathological image translation models are limited to the translation between a couple of 45 specific source-target domain pairs and their extension to the translation between multiple domains 46 . CC-BY-NC-ND 4.0 International license It is made available under a is the author/funder, who has granted medRxiv a license to display the preprint in perpetuity. (which was not certified by peer review) The copyright holder for this preprint this version posted November 1, 2024. ; https://doi.org/10.1101/2024.07.11.24310294 doi: medRxiv preprint Page 4 of 35 is not straightforward 30. Hence, simultaneous translation across multiple domains has a wider range 47 of applications in computational pathology. For example, one can edit one real histopathological 48 image into multiple synthesized versions corresponding to altered genomic or transcriptomic 49 profiles, which can potentially model the impact of mutations and altered gene expression on the 50 histopathological appearance of the tissue. Furthermore, multiple-domain translations may also 51 alleviate the inefficiency of pairwise translation, reducing the number of 𝑛𝑛(𝑛𝑛−1)/2 individual 52 pairwise models to one multi-domain model. Thirdly, current GAN-based models have specifically 53 designed components or loss functions that may hinder their extensibility to new applications 25,26. 54 Due to the above limitations, in this study, we propose a novel versatile framework, 55 Histopathological image Multi-domain Multi-omics translation with Diffusion Models (His-56 MMDM). His-MMDM achieves image translation by diffusing the source domain images into 57 noisy images distributed as Gaussian distribution and then denoising the noisy images back into 58 target domain images. Compared with previous image translation models in histopathology, His-59 MMDM stands out with two unique capabilities: (1) it can be efficiently trained to translate images 60 between an unlimited number of categorial domains (through class-conditional inputs to the model) 61 (2) it can perform genomics- or transcriptomics- guided editing of histopathological images 62 (through multi-omics inputs to the model). For categorical domain translation, we demonstrated 63 His-MMDM’s performance through four comprehensive tasks using four independent datasets, 64 including the translation of cryo-sectioned slide images to FFPE ones, virtual 65 immunohistochemical (IHC) staining of hematoxylin and eosin (H&E) images, primary tumor type 66 translation, and tumor organ site translation. Most notably, we showed through comprehensive 67 experiments that His-MMDM’s generated contents can improve the performance of recent 68 histopathological foundation models 31-33 that were mainly trained on FFPE images on various 69 discriminative and retrieval tasks. It can also facilitate histopathological knowledge transfer 70 between different tumor types and primary and metastatic tumors. Using the histopathological 71 images in The Cancer Genome Atlas (TCGA) that are equipped with the matched genomic and 72 transcriptomic profiles, we demonstrated His-MMDM’s effectiveness in performing genomics- and 73 transcriptomics- guided editing of the images. We then systematically discussed and illustrated 74 through notable examples the effect of high-frequency somatic mutations and alterations on the 75 expression level of genes/pathways on the histopathology of the tissue from a generative model’s 76 perspective. We then demonstrated the usefulness of these AI-generated contents (AIGC) by 77 showing that they are useful in educating pathologists to recognize these underlying conditions. 78 Overall, we believe that His-MMDM showcases the possibility of generic multi-domain and multi-79 . CC-BY-NC-ND 4.0 International license It is made available under a is the author/funder, who has granted medRxiv a license to display the preprint in perpetuity. (which was not certified by peer review) The copyright holder for this preprint this version posted November 1, 2024. ; https://doi.org/10.1101/2024.07.11.24310294 doi: medRxiv preprint Page 5 of 35 omic translation and generation in histopathology, provides a powerful tool for pathologists, and 80 could serve as an integral component of GenAI copilot systems in the future. 81 Results 82 The design of His-MMDM model 83 His-MMDM is trained based on the principle of diffusion models (DMs) 7 and is trained with 84 histopathological images. A DM is comprised of a forward diffusion and a backward denoising 85 process (Fig. 1). In the forward diffusion procedure, a histopathological image (𝑋𝑋𝑇𝑇) is gradually 86 diffused into a noisy image (𝑋𝑋0) following a Gaussian distribution using a pre-determined schedule 87 and in the backward denoising procedure, the noisy image is gradually denoised back into a 88 histopathological image (Methods). At each iteration of the denoising procedure, a U-net-based 89 7,34 network is used to predict noise from the image at the current iteration step, which is utilized in 90 the subsequent denoising step derived from the inversion of the forward procedure (Methods) 35. 91 Conditional generation is achieved by the addition of condition information to the U-net through 92 embedding vectors added to each layer of the U-net blocks (Fig. 1, Table S1, Methods). The DM 93 used in His-MMDM can handle three types of condition information: (1) categorical labels, such 94 as FFPE/cryosection, IHC stains, tumor types, and tumor primary sites. (2) genomics, which 95 includes the mutation status of the corresponding sample, and (3) transcriptomics, which includes 96 the transcriptomic profile of the sample. After His-MMDM is trained, it achieves image translation 97 by (1) executing the forward diffusion procedure of the trained DM by conditioning the U-net with 98 the source domain condition information and then (2) executing the backward denoising procedure 99 by conditioning the U-net with the target domain condition information (detailed in Methods). We 100 demonstrate in the following sections that such a strategy can successfully achieve multi-domain 101 and multi-omics image translation. 102 Cryosection to FFPE conversion and virtual IHC staining of histopathological images 103 We first evaluated the effectiveness of His-MMDM on previously studied image translation tasks, 104 such as converting cryosectioned slide images to formalin-fixed and paraffin-embedded (FFPE) 105 images 26, as well as virtual IHC staining 24,25,36, without any task-specific adaptation of the model 106 architecture. 107 To convert cryosectioned slide images into FFPE with His-MMDM, we trained it to conditionally 108 generate TCGA slide images of their respective slide type. We collect histopathological image 109 patches from a total of 19 tumor types from five families, including five gastrointestinal (GI) 110 (ESCA, STAD, COAD, READ, and PAAD), two lung (LUAD and LUSC), three kidney (KIRP, 111 KICH, and KIRC), three pan-gynecological (GYN) (OV, BRCA, and UCEC), and five other tumor 112 . CC-BY-NC-ND 4.0 International license It is made available under a is the author/funder, who has granted medRxiv a license to display the preprint in perpetuity. (which was not certified by peer review) The copyright holder for this preprint this version posted November 1, 2024. ; https://doi.org/10.1101/2024.07.11.24310294 doi: medRxiv preprint Page 6 of 35 types (LIHC, HNSC, SARC, THCA, BLCA, and PRAD) (Methods, statistics shown in Table S2, 113 abbreviations defined in Table S3). We ran the forward diffusion process on a cryosectioned slide 114 image under the condition ‘cryosection’ followed by the backward denoising process under the 115 condition ‘FFPE’ (Fig. 2a, Methods, Table S1). We translated the test image patches from all 19 116 tumor types in TCGA using our pre-trained model and evaluate the reduction of the Frechet 117 Inception Distance (FID) before and after translation (ΔFID=FIDbefore−FIDafter) as the metric 118 for evaluating the performance of translation (Fig. 2b). Although His-MMDM is not specifically 119 designed for this task, it achieved performance comparable to the dedicated method AI-FFPE 26 120 across most tumor types, even surpassing it in six out of 19 tumor types. In the remaining tumor 121 types where AI-FFPE has an edge, the performance gap between His-MMDM and AI-FFPE is not 122 greater than that observed with other methods. Overall, His-MMDM achieved better performance 123 compared to other general GAN-based models, CycleGAN 28 and CUT 37. For other diffusion 124 model-based image translation methods, His-MMDM achieved comparable performance as BBDM 125 38, but is much better than the few-shot translation model D2C 39. Through more detailed visual 126 examination, we found that His-MMDM can alleviate the problem of tissue dehydration and 127 nuclear deformation that are common in cryosectioned slides and generate a better presentation of 128 contrasting regions and arrangement of the histopathological contents (Fig. 2c). 129 We emphasize that translating cryosectioned slide images into FFPE images has broader 130 implications beyond merely improving image quality for pathologists' observation. Although recent 131 histopathological foundation models 16,32,33 are pre-trained on diverse multi-center cohorts, the 132 majority of their training data consists of FFPE slide images rather than cryosectioned ones. 133 Consequently, directly applying these models to cryosectioned slide images risks significant 134 performance degradation. We systematically assessed whether this degradation could be mitigated 135 by first translating cryosectioned slides into FFPE images using His-MMDM. Following a similar 136 image-to-image retrieval method as in the PLIP paper 16, we evaluated the performance of various 137 image encoders on an image-to-image retrieval task (Fig. 2d, Methods). With His-MMDM, the 138 retrieval accuracy increased by 0.30, 0.08, and 0.08 for pretrained PLIP, CLIP 40, and InceptionV3 139 41, respectively (Fig. 2e). Additionally, His-MMDM improved the text-guided classification 140 accuracy of PLIP by 0.39 (macro-averaged, Methods, Fig. S1a-b). Beyond multi-modal pre-141 trained models like PLIP, His-MMDM also enhances self-supervised image-only pre-trained 142 models, such as CHIEF 33 (Methods), on tasks such as inferring microsatellite instability (MSI) in 143 colorectal (CRC) tumors (Fig. 2f) and IDH1 mutation status in gliomas (Fig. 2g). 144 . CC-BY-NC-ND 4.0 International license It is made available under a is the author/funder, who has granted medRxiv a license to display the preprint in perpetuity. (which was not certified by peer review) The copyright holder for this preprint this version posted November 1, 2024. ; https://doi.org/10.1101/2024.07.11.24310294 doi: medRxiv preprint Page 7 of 35 For IHC virtual staining of histopathological images, we collected patch images of 13 common 145 markers used in brain tumor diagnosis spanning five glioma subtypes and five meningioma 146 subtypes from The First Affiliated Hospital of Harbin Medical University (hereafter referred to as 147 the ‘HMU-1st dataset’) (Methods, Table S2). Of the 13 IHC markers, five are routinely used in 148 glioma, four in meningioma, and four for both two (Table S4). In this dataset, the H&E slides and 149 the IHC slides of each patient are simultaneously available. His-MMDM is then trained on this 150 dataset to perform conditional generation of both H&E images as well as each of the 13 IHC 151 markers. It achieves virtual staining by running in serial the forward diffusion process conditioned 152 on the label ‘H&E’ and the backward denoising process conditioned on the label of the desired IHC 153 marker (Fig. S1c). 154 We evaluated the ‘inverted normalized FID scores’ (Methods) of the translated images in each 155 subtype of glioma and meningioma (Fig. 2h). The IHC markers into which the H&E images are 156 translated tend to have higher performance when the marker is more compatible with the brain 157 tumor type/subtype. For instance, the glioma markers (GFAP, ATRX, IDH-1, MGMT, and Oligo-158 2) tend to have higher performance in gliomas and so do the meningioma markers (EMA, PR, and 159 SSTR2) in meningiomas (Fig. 2h). The positive regions of the virtually stained images (as indicated 160 by the brown-colored diaminobenzidine (DAB) stain) are both in agreement with the marker’s 161 expected location of expression (in the nucleus or the cytoplasm and extracellular matrix) (Fig. 2i 162 -2j, Fig. S1d-S1e), as well as the corresponding glioma subtype (Fig. S1f-S1h, Section S1). 163 Knowledge transfer of histopathological images across primary tumor types 164 We then experimented with His-MMDM to translate histopathological images of one tumor type 165 to another. The categorical domains for this task are the above-mentioned 19 primary tumor types 166 of The Cancer Genome Atlas (TCGA) (Methods). Using discriminative models, previous studies 167 42,43 demonstrated high cross-classification performance on a lot of these tumor type pairs, i.e., the 168 performance of a tumor classifier trained on one tumor type and tested on another, which suggests 169 conserved tumor image features across different types of tumors. 170 We tasked His-MMDM to translate the histopathology images between each two across these 19 171 primary tumor types (Fig. 3a). We systematically evaluated the translation performance of His-172 MMDM and other baseline methods and discussed the heterogenous performance across different 173 translation pairs (Fig. S2a-S2b, Section S2). Through visual inspection, the translation process 174 generally keeps the cellular arrangement and structure of the source image while keeping the 175 contents compatible with the target tumor type. For example, colorectal adenocarcinoma (COAD) 176 usually features the glandular structures formed by the epithelial cells (Fig. 3b). When the 177 . CC-BY-NC-ND 4.0 International license It is made available under a is the author/funder, who has granted medRxiv a license to display the preprint in perpetuity. (which was not certified by peer review) The copyright holder for this preprint this version posted November 1, 2024. ; https://doi.org/10.1101/2024.07.11.24310294 doi: medRxiv preprint Page 8 of 35 colorectal images are translated to other adenocarcinomas, those structures are kept intact (Fig. 3b). 178 However, when they are translated to non-adenocarcinomas, those structures are attenuated as 179 much as possible and replaced with some target type-specific contents (Fig. 3b). 180 Translation of images from one tumor type to another enables new applications. For example, 181 suppose we have a discriminative model for tumor type A and we would like to apply it to another 182 tumor type B where such a model is unavailable. Instead of directly applying it to the images from 183 B, which is the cross-classification strategy as in Noorbakhsh et al. 42, we could first adapt the 184 images into type A and then use the model to classify or further fine-tune the model on the adapted 185 images. In this way, we could use His-MMDM to achieve knowledge transfer across tumor types. 186 We compared the performance (in terms of F1 score) of binary tumor classification models trained 187 on one tumor type (the target of translation) and cross-classifying images from another tumor type 188 (the source of translation/the goal of classification) using either the original images or the translated 189 images. The classification performance in four out of five tumor families corresponding to 12 out 190 of 19 tumor types on average showed higher performance using the translated images (Fig. 3c and 191 Fig. S2c-S2d, ‘ratio for fine-tuning = 0’). Additionally, to improve the classification models’ 192 performance, we fine-tuned the classification models using 30%, 60%, and 90% of the translated 193 images (Fig. 3c and Fig. S2c-S2d, ‘ratio for fine-tuning = 0.3, 0.6, 0.9’). The number of tumor 194 types with higher performance using the translated images increases to 13 at 60% and 15 at 90%. 195 Knowledge transfer of histopathological images between primary and metastatic organ sites 196 Metastasis of tumor is one of the leading factors that reduces a patient’s life quality and survival. 197 Therefore, the prediction of metastasis of tumors has important application values in clinical 198 diagnosis. From a histopathological perspective, tumors displaying higher levels of malignancy 199 tend to have a higher tendency to metastasize 44. Several previous studies have already attempted 200 to build discriminative models that can infer the metastatic potential of tumors from such images 201 45-47. 202 Using His-MMDM, we investigated our model’s potential application on this task but from a 203 generative model’s perspective. Again, we aim to address the following question about knowledge 204 transfer: can His-MMDM leverage the knowledge it has acquired from generating images of 205 metastatic organ sites to provide prognostic and staging capabilities for primary site images? We 206 trained His-MMDM on 475 lung tumor histopathological WSIs, including 315 from primary lung 207 tumors, 76 from lymph node metastases, and 84 from brain metastases from the Harbin Medical 208 University Cancer Hospital (the ‘HMU-C dataset’, Methods, Table S2). During training, His-209 MMDM was conditioned on the site (lung, lymph, and brain) of the tumor tissue, and during 210 . CC-BY-NC-ND 4.0 International license It is made available under a is the author/funder, who has granted medRxiv a license to display the preprint in perpetuity. (which was not certified by peer review) The copyright holder for this preprint this version posted November 1, 2024. ; https://doi.org/10.1101/2024.07.11.24310294 doi: medRxiv preprint Page 9 of 35 inference, His-MMDM was asked to translate the images from one site to another (Fig. S3a). As 211 before, we computed ΔFID between each pair of translations. The ΔFID scores are much higher 212 when translating from a non-solid organ site (lymph node) to a solid organ site (lung and brain) and 213 the translation within solid organ sites (lung and brain) tends to result in lower ΔFIDs (Fig. 4a). 214 Inspecting the original and translated histopathological images, His-MMDM keeps the cellular 215 arrangements and characteristics of the tumor subtypes during the translation processes, but the 216 tumor growth environment is modified to match the designated organ sites (Fig. 4b). 217 We systematically visualized the images translated from the primary organ site (lung) to the 218 metastatic organ sites, including the lymph node (Fig. 4c) and brain (Fig. S3b) according to their 219 Inception V3 features in the 2D tSNE space. The images located at the interior of the distribution 220 are more likely to be properly translated from the primary site characterized by the properly 221 arranged tumor cell nuclei and the more realistic background tumor environment when assessed by 222 pathologists (Fig. 4c, Fig. S3b and Fig. S3c). On the contrary, the translated images that look 223 eccentric (characterized by incorrect cell shape, infiltration, and unrealistic backgrounds) tend to 224 be located at the edge of the distribution whose appearance indicates less successful image 225 translation which could be attributed to a lack of compatibility of the primary site image with the 226 metastatic site (Fig. 4c). We trained an outlier detection model, Isolation Forest 48, on the Inception 227 V3 features of the translated images and produced an ‘inlier score’ (in the range [-1,1], -1 for the 228 most out-of-distribution and 1 for the most in-distribution) for each lung image to measure its 229 compatibility with the metastatic organ site (Fig. 4c and Fig. S3b). We speculated tumors that are 230 easy to translate to a particular metastatic site image will have a higher ‘inlier score’ (compatibility) 231 with that particular organ site. To verify this, we translated primary lung tumor histopathological 232 images (LUAD and LUSC) from TCGA to the metastatic organ sites (brain and lymph node) using 233 the trained His-MMDM model and computed their inlier scores using the outlier detection model. 234 Interestingly, we found that the inlier scores indeed displayed associations with the staging of the 235 patients. Specifically, a patient’s high brain inlier score is associated with M1 staging and an overall 236 staging of III-IV, and a patient’s high lymph node inlier score is associated with N1-N3 staging and 237 an overall staging of II-IV (Fig. 4d). Moreover, a high lymph node inlier score is associated with 238 poorer survival of the patients (Fig. 4e, p=5.6e-04). This trend also holds for the brain inlier score 239 (Fig. 4f, p=0.039). 240 Genomics- and transcriptomics-guided editing of histopathological images 241 Somatic mutations in oncogenes and tumor suppressors are the driver factors in tumorigenesis. 242 Previous studies have reported strong associations of driver mutations with histopathological image 243 . CC-BY-NC-ND 4.0 International license It is made available under a is the author/funder, who has granted medRxiv a license to display the preprint in perpetuity. (which was not certified by peer review) The copyright holder for this preprint this version posted November 1, 2024. ; https://doi.org/10.1101/2024.07.11.24310294 doi: medRxiv preprint Page 10 of 35 features, which were demonstrated through the predictive power of discriminative models 49-55. 244 More recently, pan-cancer studies have shown the identifiability of common driver mutations (e.g. 245 TP53 and PTEN) shared in multiple tumor types using deep transfer learning 56. Apart from 246 genomic mutations, several other works have been dedicated to the prediction of transcriptomic 247 expression levels from histopathological images, achieving various levels of success 56,57. 248 We explored the capability of His-MMDM to decipher these associations but from a generative 249 model’s perspective. To this end, we trained another His-MMDM model from the TCGA 250 histopathological images conditioned simultaneously on the genomic mutation profiles and the 251 transcriptomic profiles of the corresponding samples (Methods). In total, the genomic embeddings 252 of His-MMDM take into account the 334 genes from the ten common oncological signaling 253 pathways 58, and 188 other genes with high pan-cancer mutation rate or discriminative performance 254 56 in the TCGA cohort (Table S5). Similarly, the transcriptomic embeddings include 4,193 genes 255 that were included in the collection of 50 hallmark pathways of MSigDB (v2023.2) 59,60, as well as 256 an additional 268 genes that either displayed the greatest variability between tumor and normal 257 samples or have previously reported discriminative performance 57 (Table S5). 258 We first performed in silico genomic modification experiments using the trained His-MMDM 259 model. For a given histopathological image, the forward diffusion process is executed upon it 260 conditioned on its original genomic mutation profile, e.g., gene X is wide-type (WT) and gene Y 261 is mutated, and the backward denoising process is then executed conditioned on a modified 262 genomic profile, e.g., gene X is mutated and gene Y is WT (Fig. 5a). In this way, we are essentially 263 asking the model to edit the given histopathological image into a version if its gene X and gene Y’s 264 mutation statuses were as designated. 265 We first investigated the effect of single mutations among the top 30 genes with the highest 266 mutation rates across the aforementioned 19 tumor types on the appearance of histopathological 267 images. For each image, we used His-MMDM to produce one version of it when all genes are WT. 268 Then we produced a series of other images when each one of the genes is mutated. The mutation 269 of different genes resulted in modifications in different aspects of the histopathological image. For 270 example, in COAD, the mutation of APC had the most significant effect on the nuclear shape of 271 the cell, TP53 on the cellular density, and SMAD4 on the background and microenvironment (Fig. 272 S4a). To measure the visual effect of the mutation, we computed the cosine distance between the 273 WT image and each ‘mutated image’. Specifically, for each gene in a specific tumor type, the cosine 274 distances of the Inception V3 features of the two versions are averaged and normalized both within- 275 and cross-tumor (Fig. 5b). Some tumor types show the greatest overall effect of single mutations 276 . CC-BY-NC-ND 4.0 International license It is made available under a is the author/funder, who has granted medRxiv a license to display the preprint in perpetuity. (which was not certified by peer review) The copyright holder for this preprint this version posted November 1, 2024. ; https://doi.org/10.1101/2024.07.11.24310294 doi: medRxiv preprint Page 11 of 35 on their visual appearance, such as the kidney tumors (KICH, KIRC, and KIRP). While some other 277 tumor types showed greater effect within their tumor categories, such as COAD and READ among 278 the GI tumors and OV in GYN tumors (Fig. 5b). Mutations in HIPPO, NOTCH, and RTK-RAS 279 pathways tend to have the greatest pan-cancer effect on histopathology. Interestingly, these genes 280 in NOTCH and RTK-RAS are NCOR2 and ERBB4, but are not NOTCH1 and KRAS themselves 281 (Fig. 5b). The mutations of genes have a greater effect on histopathology in tumor types where 282 there were previously reported associations between them based on discriminative performance 283 reported by Fu et al. 56 (all gene-tumor pairs: p = 6.7e-05; p = 0.03 only high-frequency mutations 284 (>10%)). We repeated the experiments using structural distance (Methods) instead of InceptionV3 285 cosine distance and a very high correlation was observed between the two (Fig. S4b-S4c). We 286 applied the cell detection algorithm, Hover-Net 61, to the generated images of the genes which have 287 high mutation rates across multiple tumors. Most notably, the generated images corresponding to 288 the mutated version of APC, PTEN, TP53, and CDKN2A have a higher number of malignant cells 289 compared to their WT counterparts in COAD, UCEC, OV, and ESCA, respectively (Fig. 5c and 290 5d). Hereafter, a structural distance map for each edited image to highlight the edited regions (Fig. 291 5d, Methods). 292 We further validated the results of genomics-guided image editing by searching both the original 293 and edited images in a curated histopathological image database with accompanying genomics 294 profiling data (Methods). The database is curated to include real histopathological images that 295 represent various genomic mutations across different tumor types. We compared the original 296 images (w/o mutation) and edited images (w/ mutation), calculating the cosine distance 297 (InceptionV3 feature) between each image and its nearest neighbor in the database (Fig. 5e). For 298 the top 25 tumor type and mutation pairs where His-MMDM’s edits resulted in the most notable 299 feature changes (Fig. 5b), 18 pairs showed a statistically significant reduction in the distance to 300 their nearest neighbors (Fig. 5f). Additionally, we used these paired images (w/ and w/o mutation) 301 as educational material for pathologists, assessing their ability to recognize such mutations after 302 reviewing these examples (Methods, Supplementary Data). We observed an improvement in the 303 pathologists' accuracy following exposure to these examples of seven out of eight mutations in the 304 four tumor types COAD, UCEC, OV, and ESCA (Fig. 5g, Fig. S4d-S4e). These findings suggest 305 that the image content generated by His-MMDM aligns well with reality. 306 Apart from single mutations, we also investigated the effect of accumulating mutations on the 307 generated histopathological images by His-MMDM. For a total of 331 genes in nine oncological 308 pathways (one pathway excluded due to its low number of genes), we sorted the genes in each of 309 . CC-BY-NC-ND 4.0 International license It is made available under a is the author/funder, who has granted medRxiv a license to display the preprint in perpetuity. (which was not certified by peer review) The copyright holder for this preprint this version posted November 1, 2024. ; https://doi.org/10.1101/2024.07.11.24310294 doi: medRxiv preprint Page 12 of 35 them according to their mutation rate (from high to low, Table S4) and edited the images by 310 sequentially performing the mutation of 25%, 50%, 75%, and 100% genes in each pathway. We 311 then compared the cosine similarity of Inception V3 features of each of them to the WT version of 312 the image (Fig. S4f). Some pathways, such as Cell Cycle and WNT signaling pathways in COAD, 313 displayed continuous changes as the mutation accumulates (Fig. S4f, S4g). But more frequently, a 314 saturation effect was observed after the most significant change had already resulted from the initial 315 25% of the mutations (Fig. S4f). 316 Using a CRISPR/Cas9 mouse model, a recent study 62 has confirmed the sufficiency of loss-of-317 function mutations of five tumor suppressor genes (PTEN, TRP53 (mouse homolog of the human 318 TP53), RB1, STK11, and RNaseL) to induce prostate tumor progression and three additional 319 epigenetic factors (KMT2C, KMT2D, and ZBTB16) to initiate tumor metastasis (Fig. 5h). Among 320 them, only RNaseL and ZBTB16 are not included in the His-MMDM genomic embeddings. We 321 therefore attempted to edit the PRAD images in the TCGA cohort according to the mutation of the 322 remaining seven genes. For each image, we used His-MMDM to produce one version free of 323 mutations in all tumor suppressors and epigenetic factors (WT), and several mutant versions for (1) 324 individual mutations in the tumor suppressors and epigenetic factors, (2) combined mutations in all 325 tumor suppressors, (3) combined mutations in all epigenetic factors, and (4) combined mutations 326 in all tumor suppressors + epigenetic factors. We then computed the cosine distances of the 327 Inception V3 features of the mutated images versus the WT images (Fig. 5i). Among the tumor 328 suppressors, STK11 induces the greatest histopathological changes in the tissue histopathology (Fig. 329 5i). The tumor suppressors generally had higher impact than the epigenetic factors, which is 330 consistent the previous study that it is these factors that mainly contributed to the in situ 331 tumorigenesis of prostate tumor. The advantages of His-MMDM enabled in silico interpolation of 332 histopathological images corresponding to different mutation statuses of the tumor. As for images 333 from normal tissue, His-MMDM can infer its appearance when one or more of the tumor 334 suppressors/epigenetic factors are mutated (Fig. 5j). As for images from tumor tissue with some 335 somatic mutations, His-MMDM can traceback to its WT appearance or infer later stages of the 336 tumor with more accumulating mutations. For instance, for an image from the prostate tumor with 337 existing mutations in TP53 and KMT2D, His-MMDM can edit it into more or fewer mutations in 338 the tumor suppressors/epigenetic factors and can freely modify it into versions when the mutations 339 of tumor suppressors and epigenetic factors are applied either together or alone (Fig. 5k). 340 Besides genomic mutations, we investigated the effect of modifying transcriptomic profiles on the 341 generated histopathological images. As transcriptomic profiles are shaped by regulations of 342 . CC-BY-NC-ND 4.0 International license It is made available under a is the author/funder, who has granted medRxiv a license to display the preprint in perpetuity. (which was not certified by peer review) The copyright holder for this preprint this version posted November 1, 2024. ; https://doi.org/10.1101/2024.07.11.24310294 doi: medRxiv preprint Page 13 of 35 pathways, we first investigated the effect of the top 10 MSigDB transcriptomic pathways that are 343 most dysregulated in TCGA tumor samples (Methods). To exaggerate the effect of transcriptomics 344 on the generated images, for each histopathological image in the TCGA cohort, we used His-345 MMDM to produce one version of it corresponding to when a pathway was knocked up and another 346 one when it was knocked down. Similarly, we computed the cosine distance (Fig. 6a) and structural 347 distance (Fig. S5a) between the two versions of the image and visualized it accordingly. Most 348 notably, the manipulation of the ‘Mitotic Spindle’ pathway resulted in the greatest change in 349 histopathology in almost all tumor types (Fig. 6a and Fig. S5a). The result is not surprising as 350 tumor proliferation through mitosis usually has a high correlation with histopathological grading. 351 Targets of the transcription factor Myc (‘Myc Targets V1’) are influential in the tumorigenesis of 352 multiple GI tumors, and therefore, they display a high impact on the histopathology of COAD, 353 READ, and STAD (Fig. 6a and Fig. S5a-S5b). The manipulation of transcriptomic pathways also 354 had a greater histopathological effect in tumor types when their genes had previously reported 355 associations with histopathology based on discriminative performance reported by Fu et al. 56 356 (p=5.2e-03). Similar to genomics-guided editing, 20 out of the top 25 tumor type and pathway pairs 357 displayed a statistically significant reduction in the distance to their nearest neighbors in the 358 database curated for transcriptomics pathway up-regulation (Fig. S5c, Methods). Using these 359 images as educational materials, we also observed an improvement in the pathologists' accuracy 360 following exposure to these examples (Fig. S5d). Using Hover-Net, we observed a significant 361 reduction in the number of malignant cells when either the ‘G2M checkpoint’ (in 9/19 tumor types) 362 or the ‘DNA repair’ (in 7/19 tumor types) pathways was knocked up compared to when it was 363 knocked down (Fig. 6b-c, Fig. S5e and S5f), suggesting that His-MMDM can indeed capture the 364 effect of the activation of such pathways in limiting tumor growth potential. 365 Aside from pathway-level alterations, transcriptional changes in individual genes could also be 366 critical in tumorigenesis. Most notably, the amplification of genomic segments can directly alter 367 the transcriptomic expression level of genes through somatic copy number variations (SCNVs). 368 We investigated the five genes with high pan-cancer SCNV rates reported by Aaltonen et al. 63 that 369 are also available in the transcriptomic embeddings of His-MMDM. These include the prominent 370 oncogenic transcription factor, MYC, a core component of the PI3K signaling pathway, PIK3CA, 371 two genes from the cyclin family, CCNE1 and CCND1, and one gene from the Bcl-2 family, MCL1. 372 We used His-MMDM to produce one image with an average expression level of the genes in the 373 normal samples and compared it against another version when each of their expression levels was 374 elevated (3×s.d. above the average). Most notably, we observed a greater effect of the cyclin 375 genes, CCNE1 and CCND1, on the tumor histopathology, suggesting the SCNVs that promote the 376 . CC-BY-NC-ND 4.0 International license It is made available under a is the author/funder, who has granted medRxiv a license to display the preprint in perpetuity. (which was not certified by peer review) The copyright holder for this preprint this version posted November 1, 2024. ; https://doi.org/10.1101/2024.07.11.24310294 doi: medRxiv preprint Page 14 of 35 progression of the cell cycle may have a universal effect across tumor types (Fig. 6d, Fig. S5g). In 377 line with the results of Myc target genes in the pathway analysis, the gene MYC itself also induced 378 strong histopathological alterations in multiple GI tumor types (Fig. 6d, Fig. S5g). As before, for 379 these SCNV genes, there is also an association between their greater histopathological effect and 380 previously reported discriminative performance on their transcriptomic levels (p=0.04 overall and 381 p=0.02 if only tumor types where the genes have high amplification rates are considered). 382 The immune microenvironment of tumors plays an essential role in tumor development, subtyping, 383 and prognosis 64. Thorsson et al. systematically characterized the immunogenomic signatures in 384 TCGA and classified each tumor sample into six immune subtypes (C1-C6) based on the clustering 385 of transcriptomic profiles of key immune pathways 64. As it is reported that tumor samples in such 386 immune subtypes display distinct characteristics such as tumor proliferation and the types of 387 infiltrated immune cells, we were interested in investigating whether His-MMDM can reproduce 388 such observations. We selected the most abundant three (C1, C2, and C3) out of the six immune 389 subtypes studied by Thorsson et al. and dropped the three kidney tumor types due to their low 390 numbers of C1 and C2 immune subtypes (see Fig. 1D of Thorsson et al. 64). We then used His-391 MMDM to edit the images from one immune subtype into another one (e.g. editing C1 images into 392 C2 or C3) by manipulating the five underlying pathways that define the immune subtypes 393 (Methods, Table S5). The detection of cell types using Hover-Net on the images before and after 394 His-MMDM’s edit revealed an increase (with a few exceptions) in the number of necrotic cells 395 when translating images from C1 to C3 (in five tumor types) and C2 to C3 (in five tumor types) 396 (Fig. 6e-f, Fig. S5h) which is consistent with C3’s lower tumor cell proliferation and high 397 inflammatory response. As a negative control, we used randomly selected sets of genes (while 398 keeping the sizes of the gene sets the same) (Table S5) for the underlying immune pathways and 399 did not observe statistically significant increments in most cases (Fig. S5i). We finally demonstrate 400 through an example when His-MMDM is guided by the combination of genomic mutations and 401 transcriptomic profiles to edit histopathological images between BRAF-like and RAS-like thyroid 402 tumors. However, we delegate such discussions to the Supplementary Materials (Section S3, Fig. 403 S5j-S5k). 404 Discussion 405 In this study, we have developed His-MMDM, a histopathological image translation model that 406 translates histopathological images between multiple categorial domains or edits them guided by 407 genomic or transcriptomic profiles. A lot of previous evidence has suggested the feasibility of 408 building such general image translation models across histopathological domains. Firstly, several 409 . CC-BY-NC-ND 4.0 International license It is made available under a is the author/funder, who has granted medRxiv a license to display the preprint in perpetuity. (which was not certified by peer review) The copyright holder for this preprint this version posted November 1, 2024. ; https://doi.org/10.1101/2024.07.11.24310294 doi: medRxiv preprint Page 15 of 35 previous studies have pointed out the existence of conserved histopathological image features 410 across different tumor types 42,43. His-MMDM also demonstrates this via explicitly translating 411 images and transferring knowledge between them. Secondly, pan-cancer studies have demonstrated 412 through discriminative models the high predictive performance of the tumor origin from 413 histopathological images 65, multiple genomic mutations 56, and multiple genes’ expression levels 414 57. We demonstrated, for the first time, that such relationships can be manifested through a 415 generative model, by explicitly generating histopathological images corresponding to in silico 416 altered tumor types or genomic and transcriptomic profiles. Thirdly, several dedicated 417 histopathological image translation models have already been proposed in specific application 418 domains such as cryosection to FFPE translation and in silico virtual staining, which proves the 419 applicability of image translation in histopathology 25,26. We have shown that the general His-420 MMDM model can achieve performance comparable to these dedicated models. Most notably, we 421 illustrated that such translation from cryosection to FFPE not only improves the visual quality for 422 pathologists, but is useful to improve the performance of existing histopathological foundation 423 models as well. 424 Very recently, several new generative models to synthesize histopathological images based on 425 genomics or transcriptomics have begun to emerge. For instance, Dolezal et al. proposed a 426 conditional GAN (cGAN) based model 66 that can synthesize images of different subtypes in lung, 427 breast, head & neck, and thyroid tumors. In particular, the model can synthesize thyroid tumor 428 images from BRAFV600E-like ones to RAS-like ones. Carrillo-Perez et al. sequentially developed a 429 GAN-based 67 and a diffusion-based generative model 68 for the synthesis of histopathological 430 images from bulk RNA-seq profiles. It is worth noting that these previous works are generative 431 models conditioned only on a genomic/transcriptomic profile and synthesize new images from 432 scratch. His-MMDM, however, distinguishes itself as an image translation model that can edit 433 existing images into new ones. This property is indispensable in our analyses of the effect of 434 genomic/transcriptomic manipulations on real histopathological images. This also makes His-435 MMDM more useful in real-world applications in that it achieves multi-omics-guided editing of 436 the existing images rather than the un-constrained generation of new ones. 437 In recent years, GenAI-based copilot systems have shown great potential to revolutionize the way 438 we interact with computers 69-71. In parallel, preliminary attempts have also been taking place in 439 biomedicine and such GenAI systems are showing great potential in helping biologists and 440 healthcare practitioners 19,20,72,73. As a GenAI model itself, His-MMDM could fit naturally into such 441 ecosystems and become useful for a pathologist’s day-to-day workflow. One limitation of His-442 . CC-BY-NC-ND 4.0 International license It is made available under a is the author/funder, who has granted medRxiv a license to display the preprint in perpetuity. (which was not certified by peer review) The copyright holder for this preprint this version posted November 1, 2024. ; https://doi.org/10.1101/2024.07.11.24310294 doi: medRxiv preprint Page 16 of 35 MMDM concerns its high computational demand. Due to the iterative nature of the diffusing and 443 denoising processes of diffusion models, it still takes minutes to translate a single batch of 444 histopathological image patches on a typical machine equipped with eight NVIDIA V100 GPUs. 445 Strategies for developing more efficient diffusion models 74 can be adopted to streamline the 446 process of image translation. Experiments with more efficient diffusion model solvers 75,76 can also 447 be an interesting exploration for future works. Currently, for simplicity, we trained His-MMDM 448 with fixed resolution (128×128) under a unified magnification level in each cohort. Extending His-449 MMDM to simultaneously work at different magnification levels will further unleash its 450 application potential. 451 Methods 452 Datasets 453 In this research, three cohorts of histopathological image datasets were used to train and test the 454 His-MMDM model under different image translation settings. 455 The Cancer Genome Atlas cohort (TCGA) We downloaded 22,596 whole-side histopathological 456 images from The Cancer Genome Atlas (TCGA) web portal (https://portal.gdc.cancer.gov/) of the 457 19 cancer types following the practice of 42. The 19 cancer types contain five gastrointestinal 458 (ESCA, STAD, COAD, READ, and PAAD), two lung (LUAD and LUSC), three kidney (KIRP, 459 KICH, and KIRC), three pan-gynecological (OV, BRCA, and UCEC), and five other tumor types 460 (LIHC, HNSC, SARC, THCA, BLCA, and PRAD) (statistics shown in Table S2). These tumor 461 types in TCGA were specially chosen because they contain enough numbers of tumor and normal 462 slides, as well as enough numbers of FFPE and cryosectioned slides for analyses. The patient’s 463 metadata and clinical information were downloaded along with the WSIs. The processed genomic 464 mutations (TCGA Unified Ensemble "MC3" gene-level mutation calls) and the bulk transcriptomic 465 profiles (Illumina Hi-Seq) of the corresponding samples were downloaded from the UCSC Xena 466 website (https://tcga.xenahubs.net) 77. This cohort is used for training and testing His-MMDM to 467 translate across different primary tumor types and different genomic and transcriptomic profiles. 468 For genomic profiles, the 522 genes we considered in total include the 334 genes from the ten 469 oncological signaling pathways defined in 58, and 188 other genes with high pan-cancer mutation 470 rate or discriminative performance 56 in the TCGA cohort. We strictly followed the "MC3" gene-471 level mutation calls’ definition and only non-synonymous mutation statuses were considered for 472 each gene. For transcriptomic profiles, we considered a total of 4,461 genes that are available in 473 the TCGA transcriptomic profiles, including 4,193 genes that are included in the collection of 50 474 hallmark pathways of MSigDB (v2023.2) 59,60, as well as an additional 268 genes that either 475 . CC-BY-NC-ND 4.0 International license It is made available under a is the author/funder, who has granted medRxiv a license to display the preprint in perpetuity. (which was not certified by peer review) The copyright holder for this preprint this version posted November 1, 2024. ; https://doi.org/10.1101/2024.07.11.24310294 doi: medRxiv preprint Page 17 of 35 displayed the greatest variability between tumor and normal samples (in terms of the t-statistic) or 476 previously reported discriminative performance 56. During the evaluation of the His-MMDM model, 477 we prioritized the top 30 genes in terms of pan-cancer mutation rate from nine oncological 478 pathways for genomic-guided editing and the top 10 pathways in terms of pan-cancer t-statistic 479 from MSigDB for transcriptomic manipulation. 480 The Harbin Medical University Cancer Hospital Cohort (HMU-C) This cohort contains 475 481 primary and metastatic lung tumor histopathological WSIs of 400 patients from the Harbin Medical 482 University Cancer Hospital. This HMU-C cohort consists of 315 WSIs from primary lung tumor 483 tissues, 76 WSIs from lymph node metastases, and 84 WSIs from the brain metastases of the same 484 set of patients. The primary and metastatic tumor tissues are from the most common lung tumor 485 subtypes: adenocarcinoma, squamous cell carcinoma, and small cell carcinoma (statistics in Table 486 S2). All slides in this cohort are FFPE slides. 487 The First Affiliated Hospital of Harbin Medical University Cohort (HMU-1st) This cohort contains 488 6,200 brain tumor histopathological WSIs of 557 patients from the First Harbin Medical University 489 Hospital. Among the 6,200 WSIs, 2,660 are glioma slides, and 3,540 are meningioma slides; 2,753 490 were H&E-stained and 3,447 were IHC-stained with 14 different markers (Table S4). Some of 491 these markers are useful for the confirmation of the source of the tumor tissue, such as ATRX; 492 some are used to display specific cellular processes, such as Ki67; while others are useful in tumor 493 subtyping and forecasting prognoses, such as IDH-1 and MGMT (Table S4). Among them six 494 markers are commonly used in both meningiomas and gliomas, four are used exclusively in gliomas 495 and the other four are used exclusively in meningiomas (Table S4). 496 The protocols for the human studies comply with all relevant ethical regulations and are approved 497 by the Ethics Committee of The First Harbin Medical University Hospital and The Harbin Medical 498 University Cancer Hospital (KY2021-42). The consent forms of the patients were waived before 499 this research was carried out under the retrospective research protocol of the institutions. 500 Patch selection and patch feature extraction 501 The WSIs from each cohort were segmented for tissue regions from the empty slide background. 502 The slide image was then converted to a binary mask using Otsu’s thresholding method on the 503 Gaussian blurred version of the saturation channel. Subsequently, using a sliding window-based 504 approach, the carved-out tissue area is completely covered with image patches that are 256×256 505 (if the objective magnification is 20×) or 512×512 (if the objective magnification is 40×) in 506 size. To select patches from the WSIs that contain representative tumor tissues, we extracted the 507 . CC-BY-NC-ND 4.0 International license It is made available under a is the author/funder, who has granted medRxiv a license to display the preprint in perpetuity. (which was not certified by peer review) The copyright holder for this preprint this version posted November 1, 2024. ; https://doi.org/10.1101/2024.07.11.24310294 doi: medRxiv preprint Page 18 of 35 image patches’ features using a pretrained (on ImageNet 78) ViT-L-16 79 model. From each cohort’s 508 extracted patches, we asked the pathologists to select a small set of positive patches (which contains 509 representative tumor cells and microenvironment) and a small set of negative patches (in which the 510 tumor tissue content is less than 10% of the area covered). We then classified the patches as 511 positive/negative from each WSI based on their similarity to the positive/negative patch sets (as 512 measured by the cosine similarity of their ViT-L-16 feature vectors). From each WSI, we randomly 513 sampled a certain number of patches from the ‘positive patches’ determined above and then 514 incorporated them in the train/test set of the His-MMDM model. The patches were resized to 515 128×128 before being processed by the generative models. The statistics of the extracted patches 516 in each cohort are shown in Table S2. 517 The Multi-domain multi-omics image translation model 518 His-MMDM is an image translation model that learns a mapping ℱ:𝑋𝑋(src)→𝑋𝑋(trg) that maps a 519 source domain image 𝑋𝑋(src) to a target domain image 𝑋𝑋(trg). If the images in the source domain 520 are distributed as 𝑝𝑝src(𝑋𝑋) and the images in the target domain are distributed as 𝑝𝑝trg(𝑋𝑋), a well-521 trained image translation model is expected to have the property that ℱ(𝑋𝑋(src)) distributes as 522 � �𝑝trg(𝑋𝑋). Although it is possible to translate images between different dimensions 30, we are 523 assuming that 𝑋𝑋(src) and 𝑋𝑋(trg) to have the same dimensions in this paper for simplicity. This 524 assumption is valid since we have resized images to represent the same physical dimension when 525 they come from slides with different magnification levels. 526 Su et al. have demonstrated theoretically that such mapping ℱ corresponds to a deterministic 527 solution of the probability flow ordinary differential equations (PF-ODEs) of the Schrödinger 528 Bridge Problem (SBP) between the two distributions 𝑝𝑝src and 𝑝𝑝trg 30. The SBP from 𝑝𝑝src to 𝑝𝑝trg 529 aims to establish the most likely evolutionary path of a distribution from 𝑝𝑝src to 𝑝𝑝trg. They also 530 established the connection between the SBP and the score-based generative modeling (SGM) of 531 DMs and proved that SGM is a special case of the SBP when the evolution is from a particular data 532 distribution (could be 𝑝𝑝src or 𝑝𝑝trg) to the multivariate Gaussian distribution. In this way, such 533 mapping ℱ can be found by composing the forward (from 𝑝𝑝src→𝑁𝑁(0,𝐈𝐈)) and backward diffusion 534 processes (from 𝑁𝑁(0,𝐼𝐼)→𝑝𝑝trg) of the SGM. Concretely, during the forward diffusion processes, 535 we obtain the latent noisy image 𝑋𝑋(lat) from a source domain image 𝑋𝑋(src) with 536 � �𝑋(lat)=ODESolve(𝑋𝑋(src);𝑓𝑓(src),𝑡𝑡1=0,𝑡𝑡2=T) 537 . CC-BY-NC-ND 4.0 International license It is made available under a is the author/funder, who has granted medRxiv a license to display the preprint in perpetuity. (which was not certified by peer review) The copyright holder for this preprint this version posted November 1, 2024. ; https://doi.org/10.1101/2024.07.11.24310294 doi: medRxiv preprint Page 19 of 35 where 𝑓𝑓(src) is the denoising diffusion model trained in the SGM of the source domain, ODESolve(⋅ 538 ;𝑡𝑡1=0,𝑡𝑡2=T) represents the numerical approximation of the forward PF-ODE that maps the 539 source domain image 𝑋𝑋(src) to the latent image 𝑋𝑋(lat), and T is the preset number of discretization 540 steps of the solver. If we assume that the SGM models perfectly the source domain score function 541 and there is no discretization error in solving the PF-ODE, 𝑋𝑋(lat) should distribute as 𝑁𝑁(0,𝐈𝐈). 542 Symmetrically, we can then transform the latent image 𝑋𝑋(lat) to the target domain with 543 � �𝑋(trg)=ODESolve(𝑋𝑋(lat);𝑓𝑓(trg),𝑡𝑡1=T,𝑡𝑡2=0) 544 where 𝑋𝑋(trg) is the generated target domain image, ODESolve(⋅;𝑡𝑡1=T,𝑡𝑡2=0) represents the 545 numerical approximation of the backward PF-ODE that maps the latent image to the target domain 546 image, and 𝑓𝑓(trg) is the denoising diffusion model of the target domain SGM. 547 His-MMDM focuses on the translation of histopathological images belonging to different domains. 548 Such domains include categorical domains such as different organ sites of the tumor and different 549 types of stains, as well as multi-omic domains that correspond to different genomic or 550 transcriptomic profiles. Therefore, the denoising diffusion model used in His-MMDM needs to 551 simultaneously take care of the three types of condition information. For clarity, we first write the 552 categorical condition as 𝑐𝑐∈𝒞𝒞, where 𝒞𝒞 is a finite set containing all the possible categorical 553 conditions that are handled by this model. Secondly, we use 𝑮𝑮={(𝑔𝑔𝑖𝑖,𝑚𝑚𝑖𝑖)}𝑖𝑖=1 |𝒢𝒢| to hold the genomic 554 mutation profiles of all the genes considered by the model (denoted by gene set 𝒢𝒢) for a particular 555 train/test example, in which 𝑔𝑔𝑖𝑖 is the name of the 𝑖𝑖th gene and 𝑚𝑚𝑖𝑖 is the Boolean value of its 556 mutation status. Thirdly, we use 𝑻𝑻={(𝑔𝑔𝑖𝑖,𝑡𝑡𝑖𝑖)}𝑖𝑖=1 |𝒢𝒢| to hold the transcriptomic expression levels of 557 all the genes considered by the model, in which 𝑔𝑔𝑖𝑖 is the name of the 𝑖𝑖th gene and 𝑡𝑡𝑖𝑖 is the quantile-558 normalized value of its expression level. In this way, the source domain model 𝑓𝑓(src) and the target 559 domain model 𝑓𝑓(trg) can be obtained by providing one model parameterized by 𝜃𝜃 (𝑓𝑓𝜃𝜃) with 560 different condition information, i.e., 𝑓𝑓𝜃𝜃(⋅;𝑐𝑐(src),𝑮𝑮(src),𝑻𝑻(src)) and 𝑓𝑓𝜃𝜃(⋅;𝑐𝑐(trg),𝑮𝑮(trg),𝑻𝑻(trg)) , 561 respectively. As the denoising diffusion model is usually implemented as a U-Net architecture 34, 562 the two types of condition information are added to each layer of the U-Net model via the 563 categorical embedding and the multi-omics embedding, respectively: 564 Layer[𝑙𝑙+1]=Layer[𝑙𝑙]+𝑒𝑒cat+𝑒𝑒genomic+𝑒𝑒transcriptomic 565 The categorical embeddings are initialized randomly and trained together with the parameters of 566 the U-Net. As for the genomic embeddings, for each gene 𝑔𝑔, His-MMDM utilizes one embedding 567 . (which was not certified by peer review) The copyright holder for this preprint this version posted November 1, 2024. ; https://doi.org/10.1101/2024.07.11.24310294 doi: medRxiv preprint Page 20 of 35 for the wide-type (WT) version of the gene (𝑒𝑒𝑔𝑔) and the other for the mutated version of the gene 568 (𝑒𝑒̃𝑔𝑔) and use a multi-layer perceptron (MLP) to transform them, in other words: 569 � �𝑒genomic=� MLPgenomic�𝑒𝑒𝑔𝑔𝑖𝑖 ⋅𝕀𝕀{𝑚𝑚𝑖𝑖 is false}+𝑒𝑒̃𝑔𝑔𝑖𝑖 ⋅𝕀𝕀{𝑚𝑚𝑖𝑖 is true}� |𝒢𝒢| 𝑖𝑖=1 570 To account for the quantitative effect of transcriptomics, 𝑒𝑒transcriptomic (𝑖𝑖) is implemented as two 571 different networks (both as MLPs) that deal with genes and expression levels separately: 572 � �𝑒transcriptomic=� MLPtranscriptomic(𝑒𝑒𝑔𝑔𝑖𝑖 )×MLPexp(𝑒𝑒𝑔𝑔𝑖𝑖 ,𝑡𝑡𝑖𝑖) |𝒢𝒢| 𝑖𝑖=1 573 We first pre-train the denoising diffusion model 𝑓𝑓𝜃𝜃 using the standard classifier-guided diffusion 574 training procedure on the domains of images between which we wish to translate 80. We used the 575 Algorithm 1 in 7 for the training procedure. After 𝑓𝑓𝜃𝜃 is successfully trained, we use the denoising 576 diffusion implicit models (DDIMs) as ODESolve for image translation. The process of it can be 577 summarized as Algorithm 1. The details of the model architecture, hyperparameters and other 578 configurations regarding training and translation are specified in Table S1. 579 For comparison, we compared the performance of His-MMDM with two GAN-based models, 580 CycleGAN 28 and CUT 37. To translate images from domain X to domain Y, CycleGAN 581 simultaneously establishes two GANs where one translates images from X to Y and the other from 582 Y to X. To ensure the consistency of the translated images, a cycle-consistency loss is imposed to 583 ensure the invariance when an image is translated from X to Y and then back from Y to X. The 584 other GAN-based model, CUT, resorts to a different approach. CUT establishes only one GAN 585 model to achieve one-way translation from X to Y. It ensures consistency of translation using 586 patchwise contrastive learning 81,82 to encourage image patches of the source and translated images 587 of the same location to be similar. 588 Image translation tasks and the evaluation of the synthesized images 589 We summarize the specific settings of image translation tasks, including the specifications of the 590 model checkpoints, the dataset used the condition information of the translation tasks in Table S1. 591 The Frechet Inception Distance (FID) 83 was used to evaluate the quality of a set of synthesized 592 images against a set of real images. For baseline models (CycleGAN, CUT, D2C, BBDM, and AI-593 FFPE), we trained and tested them on the same train/test split as His-MMDM. The image features 594 after the last pooling layer of an Inception V3 model 41 were used for the computation. Let (𝜇𝜇,Σ) 595 . (which was not certified by peer review) The copyright holder for this preprint this version posted November 1, 2024. ; https://doi.org/10.1101/2024.07.11.24310294 doi: medRxiv preprint Page 21 of 35 and (𝜇𝜇0,Σ0) denote the mean and covariance of the Inception V3 features of the synthesized images 596 and real images respectively. The FID score is defined as, 597 � �𝑑FID�(𝜇𝜇,Σ),(𝜇𝜇0,Σ0)�=‖𝜇𝜇−𝜇𝜇0‖2 2+Tr�Σ+Σ0−2(ΣΣ0)1 2� 598 To quantify the effect of image translation from cryosection to FFPE, across primary tumor types 599 , and across tumor organ sites, we computed the difference of FID scores before and after image 600 translation, i.e., ΔFID=𝑑𝑑FID�(𝜇𝜇before,Σbefore),(𝜇𝜇0,Σ0)�−𝑑𝑑FID�(𝜇𝜇after,Σafter),(𝜇𝜇0,Σ0)� . 601 Although both CycleGAN and CUT are GAN-based algorithms, CycleGAN utilizes cycle-602 consistency for image translation while CUT leverages patch-wise contrastive learning81,82. Based 603 on the reported performance on TCGA, although patch-wise contrastive learning is an effective 604 objective to train image translation models between different photography domains, it seems to be 605 less effective in histopathological domains, possibly due to the repetitive nature of the contents of 606 the histopathological images which can confuse this contrastive objective. D2C 39 is an image 607 translation model based on latent diffusion. It performs image translation by using a discriminative 608 classifier to guide the sampling process within the latent space. Designed primarily for few-shot 609 translation, D2C keeps the diffusion model, encoder, and decoder fixed during adaptation to new 610 conditions. This makes it well-suited for editing images with distinct, localized regions but less 611 effective for broader image translation tasks, such as those in histopathology (Fig. 2b). Similarly, 612 BBDM 38 is a diffusion-based model that employs a latent diffusion approach. It utilizes a Brownian 613 bridge in the latent space to enable paired image translation between domains. While BBDM 614 performs comparably to His-MMDM (Fig. 2b), its paired formulation makes multi-domain image 615 translation less straightforward. 616 To compute the magnitude of the effect of genomic- and transcriptomic-guided editing, we used 617 cosine distance rather than FID as we were interested in the editing effect on a single image rather 618 than a collection of images. Besides the cosine distance of the Inception V3 deep features, we also 619 evaluated the difference between image pairs based on their structural similarity index (SSIM) 84. 620 SSIM between two image regions 𝑥𝑥 and 𝑦𝑦 is defined as, 621 SSIM(𝑥𝑥,𝑦𝑦)= (2𝜇𝜇𝑥𝑥𝜇𝜇𝑦𝑦+𝑐𝑐1)(2𝜎𝜎𝑥𝑥𝑦𝑦+𝑐𝑐2) (𝜇𝜇𝑥𝑥 2+𝜇𝜇𝑦𝑦 2+𝑐𝑐1)(𝜎𝜎𝑥𝑥2+𝜎𝜎𝑦𝑦2+𝑐𝑐2) 622 where 𝜇𝜇𝑥𝑥 and 𝜇𝜇𝑦𝑦 are the pixel mean values of 𝑥𝑥 and 𝑦𝑦, 𝜎𝜎𝑥𝑥2 and 𝜎𝜎𝑦𝑦2 are the pixel variance values of 623 � �𝑥 and 𝑦𝑦, 𝜎𝜎𝑥𝑥𝑦𝑦 is the covariance of 𝑥𝑥 and 𝑦𝑦, and 𝑐𝑐1 and 𝑐𝑐2 are constant parameters. The full SSIM 624 score of two images is computed and averaged using a sliding window approach that fully iterates 625 . CC-BY-NC-ND 4.0 International license It is made available under a is the author/funder, who has granted medRxiv a license to display the preprint in perpetuity. (which was not certified by peer review) The copyright holder for this preprint this version posted November 1, 2024. ; https://doi.org/10.1101/2024.07.11.24310294 doi: medRxiv preprint Page 22 of 35 over the two images, using the implementation from scikit-image 85. We convert SSIM into 626 ‘structural distance’ by reversing and scaling it into the range [0,1], using the formula 1−SSIM(𝑥𝑥,𝑦𝑦) 2 . 627 Both the structural distance map (per sliding window) and the structural distance score (averaged 628 for all sliding windows) are used in reporting. 629 Due to the vast difference between H&E and IHC-stained images, we evaluated the quality of IHC 630 virtual staining in a different way than the previous two tasks. Instead, we computed the ratio of 631 the FID score between two different sets of real IHC images and the FID score between synthesized 632 and real IHC images, i.e., 𝑑𝑑FID��𝜇𝜇0 ′ ,Σ0 ′�,(𝜇𝜇0,Σ0)� 𝑑𝑑FID��𝜇𝜇syn,Σsyn�,(𝜇𝜇0,Σ0)� . We refer to this as the ‘inverted normalized FID 633 score’ in the main text. In this way, both the effect of image translation and the inherent variability 634 of images of an IHC-stain can be taken into account, and the inversion makes the metric greater if 635 the synthetic IHC images are more similar to the real IHC images. 636 Enhancing the performance of pre-trained foundation models in histopathology 637 Previous studies have demonstrated the practical value of image translation models, which convert 638 cryosectioned slide images into FFPE ones, in enhancing intra-operative cryosectioned slide 639 images 26,27. However, in this work, we highlight a different perspective by showing that these 640 image translation models can improve the performance of existing pre-trained foundation models 641 in histopathology. This is especially relevant since many foundation models, such as PLIP 16, Prov-642 GigaPath 32, and CHIEF 33 have been trained predominantly on vast collections of FFPE slides 643 rather than cryosectioned ones. 644 Similar to the experiments conducted in PLIP, we performed image-to-image retrieval using image 645 features encoded by the PLIP image encoder. The retrieval process involved searching a database 646 of TCGA FFPE images from 14 sites, based on their cosine distance to the query image. Following 647 the methodology outlined by Chen et al., 2022 86, we assessed retrieval accuracy using a majority 648 voting scheme ("mMV"@k), which measures the accuracy of the predicted tumor site of the query 649 image based on the majority vote of the top-k retrieved images. To evaluate the improvement in 650 PLIP's performance on text-guided classification using His-MMDM's translated images on TCGA 651 cryosectioned slides (Fig. S1a), we had our pathologists annotate a small subset of image patches 652 from the cryosectioned slides of the TCGA colon cancer cohort, focusing on the five most abundant 653 classes in the Kather colon dataset 87. We employed prompts in the format ‘a xx histopathological 654 image,’ where ‘xx’ corresponded to one of the following: ‘adipose,’ ‘background,’ ‘epithelium,’ 655 ‘lymphocyte,’ or ‘mucus.’ The accuracy of the PLIP model was then evaluated based on the 656 . CC-BY-NC-ND 4.0 International license It is made available under a is the author/funder, who has granted medRxiv a license to display the preprint in perpetuity. (which was not certified by peer review) The copyright holder for this preprint this version posted November 1, 2024. ; https://doi.org/10.1101/2024.07.11.24310294 doi: medRxiv preprint Page 23 of 35 consistency between the model's prediction (the prompt whose PLIP embedding was closest to the 657 image embedding) and our pathologist’s annotations. 658 In contrast to the multi-modal PLIP model, the CHIEF model 33 is an image-only histopathological 659 model pretrained on vast amounts (60,530 WSIs) of real-world data. Although the dataset consists 660 of images from diverse tumor types (19 anatomical sites), they are still mainly FFPE rather than 661 cryosectioned images. We obtained the cryosectioned slide images from the colorectal tumor types 662 (COAD and READ) and the glioma tumor types (GBM and LGG) of TCGA. We then evaluated 663 the fine-tuning performance of microsatellite instability (MSI) and IDH1 mutation status prediction 664 of the pretrained CHIEF model using either the original cryosectioned images or the FFPE images 665 synthesized from them by His-MMDM. The 5-fold cross-validation results are reported. 666 Stain quantification 667 To compute the positivity of the IHC stains in the translated images, we applied the color 668 deconvolution algorithm 88 (implemented by the ‘scikit-image’ package 85) to the images and 669 obtained the intensity of the two stains components used in our HMU-1st cohort, i.e., hematoxylin 670 (as background) and 3, 3'-diaminobenzidine (DAB, as positive signal). The averaged intensity of 671 the DAB stain is used as the metric of the positivity of the patch. 672 Outlier detection of translated histopathological images 673 When His-MMDM translates an image in the source domain that is unlikely to have a 674 corresponding image in the target domain, the translated image tends to look very eccentric (Fig. 675 4c). To quantify the eccentricity of the translated images, we fitted an outlier detection model 676 (Isolation Forest 48, implemented by the ‘scikit-learn’ package 89) on the Inception V3 features of 677 them. Using the fitted model, we were able to obtain an ‘inlier score’ in the range of [−1,1] of each 678 sample, where a score close to 1 indicates that the image is a perfect inlier and a score close to -1 679 indicates that the image is likely to be an outlier. Superimposing this score on the tSNE plot of the 680 Inception V3 features of the translated patch images also shows that the patches with low scores 681 tend to be located on the edge of the tSNE structure. Using the trained His-MMDM model and the 682 fitted outlier detection model, we applied them to the TCGA LUAD and LUSC cohorts. To obtain 683 an inlier score of a WSI, we sampled 50 image patches from it and aggregated their scores by 684 averaging them. Similarly, we obtained the scores of each patient by averaging the inlier scores of 685 all their available WSIs. Finally, we analyzed the relationship between a patient’s inlier score and 686 the patient’s staging and survival information. The Kaplan-Meier method was used to estimate the 687 survival function of the patients under each condition. P values of the fitted Cox proportional hazard 688 models were reported as statistical significance values. 689 . (which was not certified by peer review) The copyright holder for this preprint this version posted November 1, 2024. ; https://doi.org/10.1101/2024.07.11.24310294 doi: medRxiv preprint Page 24 of 35 Additional evaluation of genomics and transcriptomics-guided editing of histopathological 690 images 691 In addition to qualitatively evaluating genomics- and transcriptomics-guided image editing by 692 comparing and visualizing the edited images against the originals, we implemented supplementary 693 tests to further validate their alignment with real-world data. First, we curated a database of 694 histopathological images, each characterized by a specific somatic mutation or the up/down 695 regulation of transcriptional pathways from MSigDB. These images were sourced from TCGA 696 slides that were reserved exclusively for testing and not used during model training. The selected 697 images represent regions with high epithelial tissue content, as annotated by pathologists. For each 698 somatic mutation or pathway alteration, 200 patch images were stored for each condition based on 699 whole exome sequencing and transcriptomic profiling. This database serves as a comprehensive 700 resource for examining the histopathological appearance of somatic mutations and pathway 701 changes. We then used the original (q) and edited (q̃) histopathological images as queries to this 702 database. Images ({𝑟𝑟𝑖𝑖})) from a certain condition were retrieved based on the cosine similarity of 703 their PLIP features with the query images. For the top-K retrieved images, we calculated the 704 average cosine distance to assess the overall similarity, 705 � �𝑑̅ cos(𝑞𝑞)=�𝑑𝑑cos(𝑞𝑞,𝑟𝑟𝑖𝑖) 𝐾𝐾 𝐾𝐾 𝑖𝑖=1 706 When 𝑞𝑞 is the original image without a certain mutation or pathway dysregulation and 𝑞𝑞� is the His-707 MMDM-edited image by enabling such conditions, we use the percentage reduction in this metric 708 (𝑑𝑑�cos(𝑞𝑞)−𝑑𝑑�cos(𝑞𝑞�) 𝑑𝑑�cos(𝑞𝑞) ) as the effectiveness of image editing. A higher value in this metric indicates better 709 consistency between His-MMDM-edited images to the real images in a particular genomic or 710 transcriptomic condition. 𝐾𝐾 is set to 5 in practice. 711 In addition to the evaluation using the curated database, we further utilized the two sets of images 712 generated by His-MMDM (with or without mutations or pathway up-/down-regulation) as 713 educational examples for pathologists. We randomly sampled real histopathological images from 714 the database, ensuring an equal number of positive and negative classes, and assessed the 715 pathologists' accuracy in identifying mutations and pathway dysregulations both before and after 716 reviewing the His-MMDM-generated content. 717 Statistics 718 .
